To fix (3)

To fix Ethics Approval Check

What we found

1 of 1 paper appeared to involve live data collection and lacked an ethics approval statement.

Show details

Based on the following text, we would expect an ethics approval statement, but it was not present:

It is also specifically designed for cases where different participants completed different measures for the same clinical construct with no overlapping response data, which is the assumption of common dimensionality reduction methods 14, 26, 27.

Experts were recruited among several ENIGMA working groups (i.e., ENIGMA-PD, -MDD, -OCD, -BP, -Schizophrenia, -Anxiety, -Sleep Disorders) as well as through invitations to members of the “Movement Disorder Neuropsychiatry Task Force” via circular emails.

Working Directory (setwd)

Best programming practice is to avoid setwd() in analysis code: it hardcodes an assumption about the working directory (often an absolute path on the author’s own machine), so the code breaks when run anywhere else. Keep the working directory as the caller sets it and use relative paths. No setwd() calls were found in any of the code files.

Package Installation (install.packages)

Best programming practice is to avoid install.packages() in analysis code: it installs software on whoever runs the script without asking, which is at minimum disruptive (an unexpected install, network access, a package version the author never tested against) and at worst a security risk if it points at an untrusted repository. Document required packages instead (a README, or a list of them: an renv.lock/DESCRIPTION for R, a requirements.txt for Python). No install.packages() calls were found in any of the code files.

Libraries / Imports

Best programming practice is to load all required libraries/imports in one block near the top of the code. In 18 code files, libraries/imports were at multiple places (i.e., with more than 3 non-comment lines in between).

This module runs automated data-quality checks (empty columns, SPSS filter variables, category casing, stray whitespace, numbers stored as text, problematic and colliding column names, mixed text encodings, personal information, spreadsheet formatting, and careless responding) on the extracted data files.

We examined 295 columns across 38 data files.

Issues Identified

80 file/column combinations have at least one issue.

About the personal-information flags. These are pattern matches, not confirmed disclosures, and they are tuned to over-report rather than miss something. Expect false positives:

  • Column names are matched against identifying words in English and other European languages (email, telefoonnummer, geboortedatum, …). A column called phone_number flags whether or not it holds one, and an empty or already-anonymised column flags on its name alone.
  • Values are matched against the shape of an identifier. An IP address pattern also matches a version string or a dotted numeric code; a card-number pattern requires both a real issuer prefix and a checksum, but a long numeric ID can still coincide.
  • Free text is flagged when a column holds long, mostly-distinct prose — the shape open-ended answers take. Most such columns contain no names at all.

A flag means look at this column. Where the data is already de-identified (an ip column reading Anonymized, a name column the authors emptied), no action is needed.

Spreadsheet formatting such as colour, merged cells, and blank rows/columns is not preserved when data are read programmatically or exported to CSV. Store data as a plain rectangular table (one header row, one column per variable, no colour-encoded meaning) so it is machine-readable.

Careless Responding

No file could be screened for careless responding.

What this does not cover. Only 0 of 38 data files could be screened: the rest had no detectable multi-item scale block, or too few respondents. Even in a screened file, these checks only look at multi-item rating scales — the only place where a repeated or flat answer pattern is interpretable. Single questions, open text, demographics and any item outside a detected scale block are never examined. A respondent can therefore answer most of a survey carelessly and still not be flagged here, and finding nothing is not evidence that a dataset is free of careless responding.

How to make your code run on another computer

We ran your code on a clean computer, the way a reader or reviewer would. Below is each problem we found, the scripts it affects, and how to fix it. Fix them in the order given: a problem near the top can cause the ones below it.

Files are missing from your deposit.

  • calculate_pi_pilot.py reads items-and-ratings.csv.
  • clustering_cohesion_sim-embeddings.py reads model-evaluation-input_Sep25.csv.
  • clustering-only.py reads output_similarity-analysis.

The script stops at the line that reads the file.

  1. Find the file on your computer.
  2. Add it to the deposit, in the folder where the script looks for it.
  3. If you cannot share it, for example for privacy reasons, say so in the README, and explain where readers can get it and where to put it.

Your code was written for older versions of its packages.

  • harmonize.py: “ModuleNotFoundError: No module named ‘harmonica.harmonica’”

A function or option has changed since you wrote the code, so it stops with today’s version of the package.

  1. In R, run renv::init() in your project and add the renv.lock file this creates to the deposit; in Python, run pip freeze > requirements.txt and add that file.
  2. State your version of R or Python in the README.
  3. Better still, update the code so it also works with current versions.

A package could not be installed.

  • similarity-analysis_different-models.py needs sentence_transformers.

sentence_transformers could not be installed with pip. List it with its version in requirements.txt. If it is not on PyPI, or its name there differs from the name the code imports, say in the README how to install it.

A script runs longer than our limit of 5 minutes.

  • precision-analysis_H2-H3_before-final-sample.py
  • simulation_fixed-kappa.py
  • simulation_multi-kappa.py

State in the README how long each script runs. If some steps take hours, save their results (such as fitted models) to files, add those to the deposit, and let the script read them, so readers can check the rest without waiting.

A script waits for a person to choose a file, answer a question, or give arguments on the command line.

  • create_item_dimension_mapping.py
  • engine_worker.py
  • expert-survey-analysis.py
  • local_scoring.py
  • models.py
  • multi-kappa_corrected.py
  • score_transformation.py
  • setup_encoders.py
  • similarity-analysis_theory-primed.py
  • similarity-analysis_unsupervised.py

Nobody can answer when the code is checked automatically. Write the file name into the script, and put the exact command with its arguments in the README.

Metacheck can’t automatically establish what caused the error, or how it should be improved.

  • compile-results.py
  • run_score_transformation.py
  • viz_similarity.py

Power Analysis Check

No power analyses were detected.

Power analyses need to contain the following information to be interpretable: the type of power analysis, the statistical test, the software used, sample size, critical alpha criterion, power level, effect size, and an effect size metric. In addition, it is recommended to make sure the power analysis is reproducible (by sharing the code, or a screenshot, of the power analysis), and to provide good arguments for why the study was designed to detect an effect of this size.

For an a-priori power analysis, where the sample size is determined, reporting all information would look like:

An a priori power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that for a Cohen’s d = 0.5, an alpha level of 0.05, and a desired power level of 80% required at least 64 participants in each group.

For a sensitivity power analysis, this sentence would look like:

A sensitivity power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that with 64 participants in each group, and an alpha level of 0.05, a desired power level of 80% was reached for an effect size of d = 0.5.

Preregistration Check

We found 1 preregistration.

id title template
34czw Retrospective harmonization of neuropsychiatric questionnaires in a large multi-site research consortium on Parkinson’s disease using expert mappings and semantic textual similarity OSF Preregistration

Meta-scientific research has shown that deviations from preregistrations are often not reported or checked, and that the most common deviations concern the sample size. We recommend manually checking the full preregistration at the link above, and have provided the preregistered sample size.

id sample_size
34czw Which questionnaires are included in this study is dependent on the availability of subject response data from this questionnaire for at least one participating ENIGMA-PD site. Sites were asked to share the information about questionnaire availability with us; since not all sites have shared their data yet, it is possible that for some of these questionnaires there will be no single-item response data available for the score transformation and validation phase. Based on the information that was shared with us so far from the 20 sites that signed up for this project, we have 28 different questionnaires for 6 different constructs, counting 415 items in total (see figure 2).

A precise sample size for the subject response data cannot be given at this point since not all of the data has been shared with us yet. We estimate a final sample size of N>2500 patients with Parkinson’s disease who have completed at least one questionnaire within one of the six constructs. At the questionnaire level, we expect between a few hundred and up to ~1500 participants to have data for the same instrument, while at the construct level, we anticipate that ~1000-2000 participants will have at least one measure of the same construct, depending on how frequently that questionnaire or construct was administered across the contributing studies. This anticipated sample size substantially exceeds typical requirements for confirmatory factor analysis and for Analysis of Covariance and provides adequate power for validation analyses.
Field Preregistration 1
template_name OSF Preregistration
title Retrospective harmonization of neuropsychiatric questionnaires in a large multi-site research consortium on Parkinson’s disease using expert mappings and semantic textual similarity
id 34czw
link https://osf.io/34czw
date_created 2026-01-22T21:26:59.955668
date_modified 2026-01-22T21:01:30.508518
date_registered 2026-01-22T21:26:59.908225
embargo_end_date
ia_url https://archive.org/details/osf-registrations-34czw-v1
research_questions H1 - After controlling for clinical characteristics, C-scores will not differ systematically by questionnaire source, and questionnaire will not moderate associations with clinical validators (age, sex, disease duration Hoehn & Yahr stage, motor severity, LEDD). For each dimension we will fit ANCOVA models predicting C-scores (C-scoreij = β0 + β1(age) + β2(sex) + β3(disease duration) + β4(H&Y stage) + β5(MDS-UPDRS III) + γk(questionnairek) + ∈ij) as well as extended models including questionnaire × covariate interactions to test moderation (C-scoreij = [base model] + ∑kδk(questionnairek x clinical covariates) + ∈ij). We will investigate questionnaire main effects (F-test for the omnibus effect of questionnaire, with effect size quantified as partial η²), questionnaire × covariate interactions (F-tests for each interaction term, with Benjamini-Hochberg correction for false discovery rate across dimensions), and pairwise questionnaire differences (estimated marginal mean differences between questionnaires, with 90% confidence intervals for equivalence testing).
H2 - Factor structure: We will run separate confirmatory factor analyses (CFAs) for each construct. CFA will be performed with maximum likelihood estimation, using robust standard errors if distributional assumptions are violated. The harmonized dimensional structure will be considered adequate if separate confirmatory factor analyses (CFAs) for each construct demonstrate acceptable fit indices: RMSEA ≤ .08, CFI ≥ .90, SRMR ≤ .08, and χ²/df ≤ 3.
H3 - Internal consistency: Internal consistency will be calculated with McDonald’s Omega Hierarchical (ωh) and 95% bootstrap confidence intervals (1,000 resamples) for each dimension. Harmonized dimension scores will be considered to show adequate internal consistency if McDonald’s Omega Hierarchical (ωh) ≥ .70.
H4 - Generalizability: The embedding model with the highest agreement with experts will be fine-tuned using supervised learning on our finalized item-dimension pairs. Experts will evaluate each assignment via binary rating (agree/disagree). Generalizability will be assessed through expert agreement rate (proportion of item assignments rated as “agree”) and inter-rater reliability assessed via Fleiss’s Kappa28. Inference criteria are expert agreement rate ≥ 70% (proportion of item assignments rated as “agree”) and inter-rater reliability assessed via Fleiss’s Kappa ≥ 0.60.
study_type Observational Study - Data is collected from study subjects that are not randomly assigned to a treatment. This includes surveys, “natural experiments,” and regression discontinuity designs.
blinding No blinding is involved in this study.
q5 This is an observational, cross-sectional design using existing retrospective data for secondary analyses. Blinding is not applicable to this computational harmonization study. Data collection and analysis will not be performed blind to the conditions of the experiments. The harmonization approach is designed specifically for distributed consortia that work with existing datasets where different sites have administered different questionnaires measuring similar constructs.
study_design_overview The study is designed to create harmonized symptom dimensions from diverse neuropsychiatric questionnaires collected across multiple international research sites in the ENIGMA-PD working group. The constructs and questionnaires are chosen based on the availability within the ENIGMA-PD group (see figure 2 for available questionnaires). Without harmonization, questionnaire data cannot be pooled for multisite analyses, limiting analyses to using only a fraction of the available data. To address this, our design follows a structured 8-step protocol:
1. Literature review: Review of the development, validation, and factor structures of all selected questionnaires (figure 2); inclusion of recommendations from diagnostic manuals (Diagnostic Statistical Manual (DSM-5), International Classification of Diseases (ICD)) and the Movement Disorder Society. See Supplementary Material S1 for our review. Purpose: To compile evidence on how neuropsychiatric dimensions are commonly defined and measured.
2. Dimension selection: Definition of core symptom dimensions based on the convergence of evidence across original scale publications, validation studies, expert recommendations (e.g., Movement Disorder Society), diagnostic manuals, and neuroimaging applications, along with practical considerations regarding dimension homogeneity and sample characteristics. Purpose: To establish common target psychiatric symptom dimensions that items can be mapped onto.
3. Expert mapping: Clinicians and researchers assign items to dimensions through a structured survey (see figure 1a). See our OSF repository for a PDF of the survey (https://osf.io/caxzb/files/tjy4e). Purpose: To establish expert-based item-dimension assignments as one source of evidence for integration.
4. Theory-primed semantic similarity analysis: Semantic similarity analysis using different embedding models to compute embeddings for both questionnaire items and dimension descriptions. Items are assigned to dimensions based on maximum cosine similarity to dimension description embeddings—directly mirroring the expert task. Purpose: To generate model-based item-dimension assignments as a second source of evidence for integration.
5. Integration of mappings: Integration of expert and model-based assignments; finalization of item-dimension membership. Purpose: To combine human judgment and computational methods for robust assignments.
6. Score transformation: Calculation of dimension scores for each questionnaire and transformation into standardized C-scores (see figure 1b). Purpose: To ensure comparability across questionnaires with different response formats.
7. Validation: Evaluation of harmonized scores using correlation analyses, distributional comparisons, reliability checks, cut-off comparisons and confirmatory factor analyses. Purpose: To verify that the dimensions and harmonized scores are psychometrically sound.
8. Generalization: Replication on unseen questionnaire item text with a fine-tuned embedding model; expert opinions on model item assignments. Purpose: To verify that the dimensions are generalizable. 6972963cadf1b1cabee405de 6972963badf1b1cabee405d5 6972963cadf1b1cabee405d8 6972963cadf1b1cabee405dc 6972963cadf1b1cabee405dd expert-survey_item-mapping-task pre-registration_design-table.pdf pre-registration_supplementary-material_S1.pdf figure1.jpg figure2.jpg https://osf.io/project/34czw/files/osfstorage/6972963cadf1b1cabee405de https://osf.io/project/34czw/files/osfstorage/6972963badf1b1cabee405d5 https://osf.io/project/34czw/files/osfstorage/6972963cadf1b1cabee405d8 https://osf.io/project/34czw/files/osfstorage/6972963cadf1b1cabee405dc https://osf.io/project/34czw/files/osfstorage/6972963cadf1b1cabee405dd https://osf.io/download/6972963cadf1b1cabee405de https://osf.io/download/6972963badf1b1cabee405d5 https://osf.io/download/6972963cadf1b1cabee405d8 https://osf.io/download/6972963cadf1b1cabee405dc https://osf.io/download/6972963cadf1b1cabee405dd 6ed7232116e6f9060118710cfb10659359d0eb647a1f4d6dfde1578caf4518d0 cec3a36e4cd754a329f33e604be8e2a6650cc768dd3e444a255f2b1aa333be6e 334167e159acd409a4e1fd77eb15c8313c01e5d339ee668720537dbc88328ae3 856d72846842d4db91da382ba5d9eeefd0b41233e5868638601f8feb0af1a4cb 3fe5923efb03b198c7af58ef5147361cf134741d8c5413d4be48f7644ebc746f
existing_data Registration prior to any human observation of the data
existing_data_explanation We will use subject response data from 28 different questionnaires collected across ~20 sites that are part of the ENIGMA-Parkinson’s Disease working group. The data exists but was not shared yet with the main authors of this study who will also be responsible for data analysis. The expert mapping survey is currently still running, thus no inspection of the data has been done yet.
data_collection_procedures Questionnaire items: For steps 1-5 of our protocol, we will only use the questionnaire item text/sentences but no actual response data from subjects. Which questionnaires are included in this study is dependent on the availability of subject response data from this questionnaire for at least one participating ENIGMA-PD site. Sites were asked to share the information about questionnaire availability with us; since not all sites have shared their data yet, it is possible that for some of these questionnaires there will be no single-item response data available for the score transformation and validation phase. Based on the information that was shared with us so far from the 20 sites that signed up for this project, we have 28 different questionnaires for 6 different constructs, counting 415 items in total (see figure 2).

Expert mapping survey: Experts were asked to map single items to pre-selected construct dimensions (see expert-survey_item-mapping-task file). Data collection happens via and online survey form on https://www.soscisurvey.de/, a platform for professional survey projects. Inclusion criterium was being an expert in either psychiatric or sleep diagnostics or therapies, or both.

Subject response data: For steps 6 and 7, we will use the subject response data (item scores). The study utilizes retrospective data from the ENIGMA-PD working group, which pools data from multiple international sites. Subjects eligible from the initial pool need to have a clinically assessed diagnosis of Parkinson’s Disease (PD). 6972963cadf1b1cabee405de 6972963badf1b1cabee405d7 6972963cadf1b1cabee405da 6972963cadf1b1cabee405dd expert-survey_item-mapping-task pre-registration_supplementary-material_S3.pdf pre-registration_supplementary-material_S2.pdf figure2.jpg https://osf.io/project/34czw/files/osfstorage/6972963cadf1b1cabee405de https://osf.io/project/34czw/files/osfstorage/6972963badf1b1cabee405d7 https://osf.io/project/34czw/files/osfstorage/6972963cadf1b1cabee405da https://osf.io/project/34czw/files/osfstorage/6972963cadf1b1cabee405dd https://osf.io/download/6972963cadf1b1cabee405de https://osf.io/download/6972963badf1b1cabee405d7 https://osf.io/download/6972963cadf1b1cabee405da https://osf.io/download/6972963cadf1b1cabee405dd 6ed7232116e6f9060118710cfb10659359d0eb647a1f4d6dfde1578caf4518d0 953547fe68125038de375fa7810efeab80dfbe7c73ebc9f5527e32ce095e962a 64be4f57cb01efbeb931988b5ade15033cf20ad18675ceeb2668b791327b9a95 3fe5923efb03b198c7af58ef5147361cf134741d8c5413d4be48f7644ebc746f
sample_size Which questionnaires are included in this study is dependent on the availability of subject response data from this questionnaire for at least one participating ENIGMA-PD site. Sites were asked to share the information about questionnaire availability with us; since not all sites have shared their data yet, it is possible that for some of these questionnaires there will be no single-item response data available for the score transformation and validation phase. Based on the information that was shared with us so far from the 20 sites that signed up for this project, we have 28 different questionnaires for 6 different constructs, counting 415 items in total (see figure 2).

A precise sample size for the subject response data cannot be given at this point since not all of the data has been shared with us yet. We estimate a final sample size of N>2500 patients with Parkinson’s disease who have completed at least one questionnaire within one of the six constructs. At the questionnaire level, we expect between a few hundred and up to ~1500 participants to have data for the same instrument, while at the construct level, we anticipate that ~1000-2000 participants will have at least one measure of the same construct, depending on how frequently that questionnaire or construct was administered across the contributing studies. This anticipated sample size substantially exceeds typical requirements for confirmatory factor analysis and for Analysis of Covariance and provides adequate power for validation analyses.
sample_size_rationale Sample size determination for the expert rating survey: We conducted a simulation-based sample size determination for the expert rating survey. Unlike traditional power analysis, which calculates sample size needed to reject a null hypothesis, our goal was to ensure sufficient precision and stability in Fleiss’ kappa estimates. We simulated expert ratings across a range of true kappa values (0.40–0.70) reflecting moderate to substantial agreement, and identified the number of raters needed to achieve: (1) estimation stability (marginal change <0.01 when adding one rater), (2) adequate precision (95% CI width ≤ 0.10), and (3) replication consistency (95% of replicated studies yielding kappa within 0.10 range). See supplementary material S2. for detailed results. Reproducible code for this analysis can be found in our GitHub repository.

Subject response data: We ran a simulation-based power analyses to determine sensitivity for detecting questionnaire effects in ANCOVA models (see Supplementary Material S3). With expected sample sizes of N ≈ 1500 for depression, anxiety, and sleep constructs, power exceeds 85% to detect partial η² = .02 (threshold for a small effect indicating harmonization concerns) and exceeds 95% to detect partial η² = .05 (threshold for a moderate effect indicating clear harmonization failure). For constructs with smaller expected samples (psychosis, apathy; N ≈ 800), power to detect η² = .02 is approximately 75%. Type I error rates were confirmed at the nominal 5% level. These analyses demonstrate adequate sensitivity to detect meaningful questionnaire effects. Impulse control is excluded from H1 testing as only one questionnaire (QUIP-RS) measures this construct. Reproducible code for this analysis can be found in our GitHub repository. Once all subject data is shared with us, we will run sensitivity analyses using the final sample size.
Following analytical methods for precision of reliability coefficients, we calculated expected 95% confidence interval widths for McDonald’s ωh. With expected sample sizes and 3-56 items per dimension, CI widths range from 0.004 to 0.021, providing adequate precision to distinguish acceptable (ωh ≥ 0.70) from inadequate reliability. Dimensions with fewer than 5 items will additionally report Cronbach’s alpha given potential instability of omega estimates with very few items. Reproducible code for this analysis can be found in our GitHub repository. Once all subject data is shared with us and the expert item-mapping survey is completed, we will run precision analyses using the final sample size and item counts.
measured_variables Please see attached table 2 for the overview of all measured variables. 6972963cadf1b1cabee405d8 6972963cadf1b1cabee405db pre-registration_supplementary-material_S1.pdf pre-registration_table-2.pdf https://osf.io/project/34czw/files/osfstorage/6972963cadf1b1cabee405d8 https://osf.io/project/34czw/files/osfstorage/6972963cadf1b1cabee405db https://osf.io/download/6972963cadf1b1cabee405d8 https://osf.io/download/6972963cadf1b1cabee405db 334167e159acd409a4e1fd77eb15c8313c01e5d339ee668720537dbc88328ae3 58e400896b40f4c4bffa00ff435ba5a242e0a40dd030bf93b12b9d8aed2ef000
statistical_tests 1. Literature review: No statistical models. See supplementary material S1 for our literature review.
2. Dimension selection: No statistical models. The dimension structure for all constructs was determined a priori based on literature review, theoretical considerations, and expert consensus on item wording alone, without including participant response data. Please see supplementary material S1 for details on how the dimensions for each construct were derived. The target dimensions are shown in Table 2.
3. Expert mappings: For each item i, we will calculate Pi, the extent to which raters agree for the i-th element (i.e., how many rater-rater pairs are in agreement, relative to the number of all possible rater-rater pairs). Items will be assigned to the dimension with the highest Pi. Together with Pj, the proportion of all assignments which were to the j-th category, we will calculate Fleiss’ kappa (κ_F) to quantify overall inter-rater reliability (see full Fleiss’ κ formula in supplementary material S2).
4. Semantic Similarity Analysis and unsupervised clustering: We will be testing high-performance general models (BGE-large, E5-large, Instructor, GTR-T5, MPNet, MiniLM, SimCSE, BERT, SBERT-similarity), specialized clinical models (ClinicalBert, Bio_ClinicalBERT, ClinicalSentenceTransformer, BioBERT), and mental-health specific models (Harmony, MentalRoberta) to obtain embeddings and cosine similarity scores for the single items. We adopt a theory-primed assignment approach that directly mirrors the expert task: For each embedding model, we will:
Embed dimension descriptions: Using the same dimension descriptions provided to experts in the survey (see table 2 as well as the PDF of the expert survey in our OSF repository: https://osf.io/caxzb/files/osfstorage/69247b87b8168ca2f7b54eb5), we compute embeddings for each dimension description.
Embed questionnaire items: Using the same model, we compute embeddings for all questionnaire items.
Assign items to dimensions: Each item is assigned to the dimension whose description embedding has the highest cosine similarity to the item embedding. This produces a model-based dimension assignment for every item.
This theory-primed approach directly parallels what experts do: read dimension descriptions, then assign items based on semantic fit. We can examine the similarity scores to understand why an item was assigned to a particular dimension and enables generalization to new questionnaires (Step 8), as the fine-tuned model will learn to map items to these same dimension descriptions.
5. Integration of mappings: Following the cluster analysis, we will assess agreement between expert-based and data-driven clustering, select the best-performing model, and determine final item-to-dimension assignments using a combined evidence approach.
5.1.: Agreement metrics for model selection: We will calculate two complementary metrics to evaluate and select the best-performing embedding model: Cohen’s kappa for overall item-level agreement and and per-dimension accuracy for dimension-level performance.
Cohen’s kappa: For each embedding model and each construct, we will compare expert-assigned dimensions with model-assigned dimensions across all items. For each item i, we compare its expert-assigned dimension (Eᵢ, the dimension with highest expert consensus) with its model-assigned dimension (Mᵢ, the dimension whose description has highest cosine similarity to the item embedding). Cohen’s κ measures agreement between these two classifications across all n items within a construct, where n = total number of items, K = number of dimensions, Eᵢ ∈ {1, 2, …, K}, and Mᵢ ∈ {1, 2, …, K}. This produces one kappa value per model per construct. Interpretation thresholds: κ <= 0.40 (poor), 0.40-0.59 (moderate), 0.60-0.79 (substantial), ≥ 0.80 (almost perfect).
Per-dimension accuracy: For each expert-defined dimension k, we calculate the proportion of items assigned to dimension k by experts that were also assigned to dimension k by the model. Per-dimension accuracy = (items correctly assigned to dimension k) / (total expert-assigned items to dimension k). We report overall accuracy (proportion of all items correctly assigned) and minimum per-dimension accuracy (lowest accuracy across all dimensions).
The embedding model with the highest Cohen’s kappa (averaged across constructs or construct-specific) will be selected (primary criterion, minimum threshold: κ ≥ 0.40). If multiple models have κ within 0.05 of the highest κ value, we select the model with the highest minimum dimension cohesion (secondary criterion). If no model achieves κ ≥ 0.40, we will report this as a limitation and proceed with expert-only assignments.
5.2.: Final item-to-dimension assignment using combined evidence scores: Using the selected model, we will calculate a combined evidence score for each item-dimension pair that integrates both expert consensus and cluster-based evidence: For each item i and each dimension k:
Expert evidence (E_ik): Proportion of experts who assigned item i to dimension k (range: 0-1).
Model evidence (M_ik): Normalized similarity score between item i and dimension k’s description. Raw cosine similarities are converted to a probability distribution using softmax normalization across all dimensions, ensuring M_ik values sum to 1 and are comparable to expert proportions. (Example: If item i has cosine similarities of 0.75 to “Mood & affective symptoms”, 0.60 to “Cognitive & self-perception”, and 0.45 to other dimensions, the softmax-normalized M_ik values might be approximately 0.45, 0.30, and 0.25 respectively.) Combined evidence score (S_ik): S_ik = (w_E × E_ik) + (w_M × M_ik), where w_E = 0.60 (weight for expert evidence) and w_M = 0.40 (weight for model evidence) to reflect greater confidence in expert judgment while still incorporating computational semantic similarity evidence. For each item, we will calculate S_ik for all possible dimensions k and identify the dimension with highest combined score: k* = argmax_k(S_ik). If S_ik* ≥ 0.40: Assignment of item i to dimension k. If S_ik <= 0.40: Low overall evidence; item is flagged for exclusion from dimension scores. If two dimensions have S scores within 0.05 of each other, the assignment is labelled as ambiguous and presented to an expert panel for review.
Sensitivity analysis: To evaluate robustness of the weighting scheme, we will test alternative weights: 0.70 expert / 0.30 model (more expert-weighted), 0.50 expert / 0.50 model (equal weighting), 0.80 expert / 0.20 model (heavily expert-weighted). We will report the proportion of items whose final dimension assignment changes under different weighting schemes.
The finalized item-to-dimension mappings will be used for calculating dimension scores (Step 6) and validation analyses (H1-H3). The selected model will be fine-tuned using these final mappings for generalization testing (Step 8/H4).
6. Score transformation: We will calculate dimension scores for each questionnaire and transform them into standardized scores to enable cross-questionnaire comparability. For each participant and each dimension we will identify which items from their completed questionnaire were assigned to that dimension and sum the responses to those items to create a raw dimension score (the number of items contributing to each dimension score varies by questionnaire, e.g., 4 items from BDI, 2 items from HADS, 5 items from GDS for the “Mood & Affective Symptoms” dimension; see figure 1b). Since dimension scores are composed of different numbers of items with varying response formats (e.g., 3-point vs. 5-point Likert scales, binary responses), we will transform raw dimension scores into comparable standardized scores using the following steps:
6.1.: We will calculate the maximum possible dimension score for each questionnaire: Maximum score = (number of items assigned to this dimension) × (maximum response value per item) (Example: If BDI contributes 4 items to “Mood & Affective” dimension with 0-3 response scale, maximum = 4 × 3 = 12).
6.2.: We will calculate a proportion score: Proportion score = (participant’s raw dimension score) / (maximum possible dimension score for their questionnaire); this normalizes scores to a 0-1 range. We will then transform the proportion score to C-scores (cumulative proportion scores) by which each participant receives a C-score for each dimension measured by their questionnaire.
7. Validation:
H1 - After controlling for clinical characteristics, C-scores will not differ systematically by questionnaire source, and questionnaire will not moderate associations with clinical validators (age, sex, disease duration Hoehn & Yahr stage, motor severity, LEDD). For each dimension we will fit ANCOVA models predicting C-scores (C-scoreij = β0 + β1(age) + β2(sex) + β3(disease duration) + β4(H&Y stage) + β5(MDS-UPDRS III) + γk(questionnairek) + ∈ij) as well as extended models including questionnaire × covariate interactions to test moderation (C-scoreij = [base model] + ∑kδk(questionnairek x clinical covariates) + ∈ij). We will investigate questionnaire main effects (F-test for the omnibus effect of questionnaire, with effect size quantified as partial η²), questionnaire × covariate interactions (F-tests for each interaction term, with Benjamini-Hochberg correction for false discovery rate across dimensions), and pairwise questionnaire differences (estimated marginal mean differences between questionnaires, with 90% confidence intervals for equivalence testing).
H2 - Factor structure: We will run separate confirmatory factor analyses (CFAs) for each construct. CFA will be performed with maximum likelihood estimation, using robust standard errors if distributional assumptions are violated. The harmonized dimensional structure will be considered adequate if separate confirmatory factor analyses (CFAs) for each construct demonstrate acceptable fit indices: RMSEA ≤ .08, CFI ≥ .90, SRMR ≤ .08, and χ²/df ≤ 3.
H3 - Internal consistency: Internal consistency will be calculated with McDonald’s Omega Hierarchical (ωh) and 95% bootstrap confidence intervals (1,000 resamples) for each dimension. Harmonized dimension scores will be considered to show adequate internal consistency if McDonald’s Omega Hierarchical (ωh) ≥ .70.
8. Generalization:
H4 - Generalizability: The embedding model with the highest agreement with experts will be fine-tuned using supervised learning on our finalized item-dimension pairs. Experts will evaluate each assignment via binary rating (agree/disagree). Generalizability will be assessed through expert agreement rate (proportion of item assignments rated as “agree”) and inter-rater reliability assessed via Fleiss’s Kappa. Inference criteria are expert agreement rate ≥ 70% (proportion of item assignments rated as “agree”) and inter-rater reliability assessed via Fleiss’s Kappa ≥ 0.60. 6972963cadf1b1cabee405de 6972963cadf1b1cabee405d8 6972963cadf1b1cabee405db expert-survey_item-mapping-task pre-registration_supplementary-material_S1.pdf pre-registration_table-2.pdf https://osf.io/project/34czw/files/osfstorage/6972963cadf1b1cabee405de https://osf.io/project/34czw/files/osfstorage/6972963cadf1b1cabee405d8 https://osf.io/project/34czw/files/osfstorage/6972963cadf1b1cabee405db https://osf.io/download/6972963cadf1b1cabee405de https://osf.io/download/6972963cadf1b1cabee405d8 https://osf.io/download/6972963cadf1b1cabee405db 6ed7232116e6f9060118710cfb10659359d0eb647a1f4d6dfde1578caf4518d0 334167e159acd409a4e1fd77eb15c8313c01e5d339ee668720537dbc88328ae3 58e400896b40f4c4bffa00ff435ba5a242e0a40dd030bf93b12b9d8aed2ef000

missing_data_handling As this is a multi-site retrospective consortia, missing data is not random. It is often structured, for example certain instruments not used at some sites, selective missingness in severe or cognitively impaired patients. This way, sensitivity analyses can be conducted to evaluate the impact of missingness across sites and constructs. Also, items with high missingness or low inter-site availability can be excluded from the final harmonized dimension scores.

Lakens, Daniël (2024). “When and How to Deviate From a Preregistration.” Collabra: Psychology, 10(1), 117094. doi:10.1525/collabra.117094.

Replication Check
