Validation of harmonized scores H1 -Cross-questionnaire equivalence remained incomplete in ENIGMA-PD Residual questionnaire differences remained in ENIGMA-PD, whereas no significant questionnaire effects were detected across the five depression dimensions in FRAME-DT. We tested whether the averaged CDF score differed across questionnaires for each constructdimension pair and expected a non-significant questionnaire effect for successful crossquestionnaire alignment. ENIGMA-PD cohort: Omnibus ANCOVAs controlling for age, sex, site, group, LEDD, HY stage, disease duration, and UPDRS-III total score revealed 5/14 pairs with no significant questionnaire differences (all p>.05 after FDR correction; see Supplementary Table T3). To include more construct-dimension pairs and more participants, we also ran the ANCOVA with fewer covariates, using only age, sex, site, language, and group, yielding similar results (see Table 3).

FRAME-DT cohort:

Applying the same ANCOVA framework to the Frankfurt cohort, questionnaire effects were non-significant for all five depression dimensions after FDR correction. See Supplementary Table T4 for test results per sample per group.

H2 -Structural validity was limited across instruments

The proposed dimension structure showed adequate fit for only a minority of questionnaire models in either cohort. Confirmatory factor analysis (CFA) assessed whether response patterns supported the theoretically defined dimensions within each questionnaire.

ENIGMA-PD cohort: CFAs were estimated for 15 questionnaire-construct models. Only 2 of 15 models showed adequate fit using standard cut-offs (RMSEA ≤ .08, CFI ≥ .90) 29 : HADS depression (RMSEA = .000, CFI = 1.00) and NMSQuest sleep (RMSEA = .022, CFI = .99). The remaining questionnaires showed marginal to poor fit (CFI range: .68-.99; RMSEA range: .066-.135).

FRAME-DT cohort:

We estimated CFA for seven questionnaire-construct models. Two of seven models showed adequate fit: MADRS (RMSEA = 0.028, CFI = 0.991) and PHQ-9 (RMSEA = 0.055, CFI = 0.966). HAMD was borderline (RMSEA = 0.044, CFI = 0.886), with CFI marginally below the 0.90 threshold. The remaining models showed poor fit, most severely IDS-CR (RMSEA = 0.124, CFI = 0.426). See Supplementary Table T5 for all results.

H3 -Internal consistency varied by instrument and dimension

Internal consistency was adequate for a majority of questionnaire-dimension pairs, but varied substantially across instruments and dimensions. We estimated McDonald's ω to assess how consistently items mapped to the same dimension measured a shared attribute.

ENIGMA-PD cohort: 22 of 35 pairs (63%) showed adequate internal consistency (ω ≥ .70), with ω ranging from .148 (STAI cognitive anxiety) to .931 (RBDSQ REM sleep behavior), and a median ω of .801. Poor consistency appeared in dimensions with very few mapped items (< 4 items) or heterogeneous item content spanning multiple facets of the broader dimension.

FRAME-DT cohort:

Eight of 15 (53%) met the adequacy threshold (ω ≥ .70), with ω ranging from .189 (IDS-CR somatic and vegetative symptoms) to .888 (GHQ-28 depression cognitive and selfperception), and a median ω of .703. Poor consistency was largely concentrated in IDS-CR (3 of 5 dimensions below threshold, including ω = .189 for somatic) and HAMD somatic symptoms (ω = .453). The overall replication of adequate consistency in most focused instruments, alongside low consistency in heterogeneous ones, is consistent with the ENIGMA-PD pattern. See Supplementary Table T6 for all results.

Transformation retained substantial correlation with symptom rankings

We tested whether distributional alignment altered participants' relative scores or the clinical group signal. Transformation retained substantial, but variable, correspondence with withinquestionnaire symptom rankings, while PD-HC effect sizes were virtually unchanged.

ENIGMA-PD cohort: Spearman correlations between proportion scores and averaged-CDF scores per questionnaire-dimension pair, ranged from ρ = .346 to .938 (median ρ = .75) across questionnaires with multiple mapped dimensions per construct (n = 47 pairs); questionnaires mapping only a single dimension per construct trivially achieved ρ = 1.00 (n = 10 pairs). Lower correlations reflected intentional cross-questionnaire realignment in dimensions covered by multiple instruments rather than a distortion of clinical information. Consistent with this interpretation, PD vs. HC effect sizes (Cohen's d) were virtually identical before and after transformation (Pearson r = .99 across all questionnaire-dimension pairs), confirming that the CDF transformation preserved the clinical group signal.

FRAME-DT cohort: Across 27 questionnaire-dimension pairs, Spearman correlations between

proportion scores and averaged-CDF scores ranged from ρ = .386 (HAMD anxiety and distress dimension) to ρ = .935 (GHQ-28 depression cognitive and self-perception), with a median of ρ = .737. All 27 correlations were statistically significant (all p < .001). The overall pattern mirrors the ENIGMA-PD results, confirming that the CDF transformation preserves individual symptom burden rankings within instruments while aligning cross-instrument means. See Supplementary Table T7 for all results.

Harmonized scores detected clinical group differences

To assess nomological validity, we tested whether scores related to clinical group status in the expected direction. Harmonized scores detected expected clinical group differences, with greater consistency across instruments in FRAME-DT than in ENIGMA-PD.

ENIGMA-PD cohort: Demographic-adjusted regressions showed higher scores in PD than HC for most depression and sleep dimensions. Depression somatic and vegetative symptoms showed the largest group differences (BDI: β = .170, p < .001; HADS: β = .169, p < .001). Daytime sleepiness and nocturnal sleep disturbances were significantly higher in PD (ESS: β = .046, p < .001; MDS-UPDRS I: β = .126-.138, p < .001). Results were heterogeneous across questionnaires for several dimensions (Cochran's I² > .85), consistent with the differing construct coverage across instruments.

FRAME-DT cohort:

All 29 MDD vs. HC models were significant after FDR correction (all p ≤ .002), with MDD patients scoring substantially higher than healthy controls across all depression and anxiety dimensions and all instruments. Effect sizes were uniformly large: Cohen's d ranged from +0.83 (HAMD anxiety and distress) to +1.81 (PHQ-9 mood and affective symptoms), with a median d = +1.61. The pattern was highly consistent across instruments within each dimension, further confirming sensitivity of the harmonized scores to clinically meaningful group differences.

See Supplementary Table T8 for all results.

Most questionnaire pairs showed convergent prediction

This analysis assessed whether instruments provided convergent information about shared dimensions in participants who completed more than one questionnaire. Scores from different questionnaires predicted one another for most eligible within-construct comparisons in both cohorts.

ENIGMA-PD cohort: 19 of 23 pairs (83%) with sufficient participant overlap (n ≥ 30) yielded significant cross-prediction (p < .05). Among significant pairs, ΔR² ranged from .005 to .756 and Spearman r from .07 to .889. The strongest cross-prediction was observed for nocturnal sleep disturbances (MDS-UPDRS I vs. SCOPA-SLEEP: r = .875, ΔR² = .756; RBDSQ vs. SCOPA-SLEEP: r = .728, ΔR² = .474), and REM sleep behavior and dreaming (PDSS-2 vs. RBDSQ: r = .742, ΔR² = .545).

FRAME-DT cohort:

Cross-prediction was significant for 53 of 60 eligible pairs (88%; p < .05), with Spearman r ranging from .275 to .820 and ΔR² from .073 to .624 among significant pairs. The strongest predictions were observed in depression for the mood (BDI × PHQ-9: r = .820, ΔR² = .584; HAMD × MADRS: r = .794, ΔR² = .580) and the activity and interest deficits dimensions (IDS-CR × BDI: r = .690, ΔR² = .461). Non-significant pairs were concentrated in the anxiety and distress dimension (4 of 7 pairs), likely reflecting that this depression subdimension is inconsistently operationalized across instruments. These results closely replicate the ENIGMA-PD pattern, extending cross-questionnaire convergence evidence to an independent clinical MDD sample. See Supplementary Table T9 for all results.

Harmonized scores predicted disability

To assess utility beyond questionnaire agreement and clinical group differences, we predicted WHODAS 2.0 disability scores using leave-one-out cross-validation (LOO-CV). Harmonized scores predicted overall disability in FRAME-DT, although prediction varied across functional domains. Averaged-CDF scores significantly predicted total disability (LOO-CV R² = .394 versus baseline R² = -.078). Prediction was also significant for cognition and communication (R²_LOO = .248), participation (R²_LOO = .225), and mobility (R²_LOO = .132), but near zero for social participation and household activities (R²_LOO = .019). Somatic and functional depression items predicted disability better than core mood items.

Evaluation of automated item-dimension mapping

Unlike the preceding analyses of participant scores, this evaluation tested whether automated models could reproduce the experts' semantic mapping decisions. General-purpose embedding models agreed more closely with expert item-dimension assignments than clinical and mentalhealth-specific models. Across 14 models evaluated against Round 1 assignments, the bestperforming base models were gtr-t5 for anxiety (κ = .788), bge-large for apathy (κ = .647) and depression (κ = .658), SBert-similarity for impulse control (κ = 1.000), instructor for psychosis (κ = 1.000), and SimCSE for sleep (κ = .690; Figure 2). Clinical and mental-health-specific models generally showed lower agreement, with several performing near or below chance for apathy and sleep (κ range: .009-.431). Per-dimension accuracy and expert-model disagreements are reported in Supplementary Material S6; similarity scores for the best-performing models are provided in Supplementary Table T10. We also checked whether the model was simply matching keywords rather than understanding meaning. For each item, we compared the model's choice to a simple keyword-matching baseline.

The two approaches agreed only 35% of the time overall, showing that the model is not just keyword matching. Word overlap correlated more strongly with similarity margins (r = .47) than with classification correctness (r = .21), suggesting that lexical overlap influenced confidence more than accuracy. Finally, item length was unrelated to either outcome.

Fine-tuning improved agreement with experts

We fine-tuned the best-performing base model per construct using contrastive learning (five constructs) or a prototype approach (impulse control). After fine-tuning on Round 1 of expert assignments, models showed consistent improvement in Cohen's κ and accuracy over their base counterparts across all constructs (Δκ = .059-.308 on Round 2 cross-validation), with the largest gains for anxiety (+.308) and depression (+.178). See Supplementary Table 11 for all detailed cross-validation results.

Models generalized to new questionnaire items

Fine-tuned models achieved 85.3% accuracy on new items, supporting extension of the mapping approach to questionnaires outside the training set. We assessed generalization by applying the fine-tuned models (trained on Round 1 item-mappings) to 347 new items and their mappings from expert survey Round 2. We chose Round 2 questionnaires based on online availability, open licensing, and widespread adoption, and we do not have any participant response data for those in the ENIGMA-PD working group. However, two of the Round 2 questionnaires are available in the FRAME-DT cohort, one of them was included in the expert survey Round 2 (MADRS) and for the other one (GHQ-28) we used the final depression model to predict item-dimension mappings.

Overall, fine-tuned models achieved almost perfect agreement with Round 2 expert labels (κ = .817 [.770, .860]). Agreement was almost perfect for impulse control (κ = .884 [.821, .941]) and anxiety (κ = .877 [.740, .970]), substantial for depression (κ = .746 [.658, .824]) and psychosis (κ = .700 [.388, 1.000]), and moderate for sleep (κ = .468 [.125, .727]). Fine-tuned models consistently outperformed their untuned base counterparts on Round 2 items across all constructs (Δκ = .059-.308), demonstrating that contrastive fine-tuning on expert judgments meaningfully improves generalization to novel items (see Figure 3).

Methods

Literature review and dimension selection

The goal of selecting dimensions was to find common ground on which the different questionnaires could be harmonized. We reviewed factor analysis and validation studies, recommendations on neuropsychiatric symptoms in PD of the Movement Disorder Society (MDS) task force, recommendations from diagnostic manuals (Diagnostic Statistical Manual (DSM-5) 30 , International Classification of Diseases (ICD-11) 31 ), neuroimaging findings and other clinical studies supporting distinct symptom dimensions. After the literature review, a panel of N=4 experts (JKP, EvH, OvdH, YvdW) made the final decision on the dimensions after three separate discussion rounds. See Supplementary Material S1 for further details on the process and the full literature review. The following dimensions per construct were chosen (Table 4).

Construct Dimensions

Anxiety 1. Somatic Symptoms 2. Cognitive Anxiety Depression 1. Mood & Affective 2. Cognitive & Self-Perception 3. Somatic & Vegetative 4. Activity & Interest 5. Anxiety & Distress Psychosis 1. Hallucinations 2. Delusions Apathy 1. Behavioral 2. Cognitive 3. Affective Impulse Control Disorders 1. Pathological Gambling 2. Hypersexuality 3. Compulsive Buying 4. Compulsive Eating 5. Punding/Hobbyism 6. Dopamine dysregulation syndrome Sleep 1. Daytime Sleepiness & Alertness 2. Nocturnal Sleep Disturbances 3. REM Sleep Behaviour and Dreaming 4. Sleep-Disordered Breathing

Table 4. The six neuropsychiatric constructs included in this study and their dimensions based on the literature review. No subject response data were used in this process.

Expert survey

We conducted a two-round expert survey to establish ground-truth dimension assignments for psychiatric questionnaire items across the six neuropsychiatric constructs. In each round, expert raters were presented with the text of individual questionnaire items alongside the theoretically defined dimension descriptions for each construct. Raters assigned each item to the dimension it best captured (forced 1-choice), or indicated "does not fit" if the item did not map to any dimension.

See all questionnaires and items included in the expert surveys in Supplementary Table T1. and our OSF repository for a PDF excerpt of the survey (https://osf.io/caxzb/files/tjy4e). We selected the questionnaires based on availability in the ENIGMA-PD working group for Round 1 questionnaires (high-priority questionnaires for ENIGMA-PD projects). For Round 2 questionnaires we relied on online availability, open licensing, and widespread adoption. Item presentation was not randomized, but items were given in the order as they appear in the questionnaire. Questionnaires were visually separated. Expert confidence for each item was quantified using the probability 𝑃_𝑖 that two randomly selected raters would agree, 𝑃_𝑖 = 𝛴 𝐸²ᵢₖ, where 𝐸_𝑖𝑘 is the proportion of raters assigning item 𝑖 to dimension 𝑘. Inter-rater reliability was assessed using Fleiss' κ per construct 32 , with 95% confidence intervals. Beforehand, we ran simulation analyses to determine how many raters per construct and per κ value we needed to achieve a reliable κ estimate (see Supplementary Material S2).

Experts were recruited among several ENIGMA working groups (i.e., ENIGMA-PD, -MDD, -OCD, -BP, -Schizophrenia, -Anxiety, -Sleep Disorders) as well as through invitations to members of the "Movement Disorder Neuropsychiatry Task Force" via circular emails. Experts in Round 2 were the same as in Round 1, though we split the surveys into shorter versions and distributed these different versions among the experts; thus, not every expert from Round 1 completed the same survey in Round 2. We did this 1) out of courtesy to the experts' time and 2) because Round 2 served as a validation dataset only. Experts did not receive financial compensation for their participation but were given the opportunity to join as co-authors of this manuscript.

Raters who demonstrated systematic low agreement (𝑃_𝑖) with other raters (3 SD threshold) were excluded (N=2 in each round), and items with low inter-rater agreement (𝑃_𝑖 < 0.40) were flagged for review (expert review round of N=3, JKP, EvH, OdvH). Items mapped "does not fit" were not included in any dimension and were excluded from further analyses. We determined the final item-to-dimension assignments based on 𝑃_𝑖.

Score transformation

We transformed raw questionnaire responses into a common scale to enable cross-questionnaire comparison (see Figure 4 for a schematic overview of the transformation pipeline). In short, we summed raw item responses per dimension and divided by the maximum possible score to obtain a proportion score on [0, 1]. We then mapped each questionnaire's proportion scores through its own empirical cumulative distribution function (CDF) to obtain within-questionnaire percentile ranks. Finally, these percentile ranks were re-expressed on the scale of the questionnaireaveraged CDF, which is a weighted average of all per-questionnaire CDFs (equal weight per questionnaire). This yielded the averaged CDF score (𝑎𝑣_𝑐𝑑𝑓_𝑠𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑒). This score is directly comparable across instruments: two participants with the same within-questionnaire percentile rank receive the same 𝑎𝑣_𝑐𝑑𝑓_𝑠𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑒 regardless of which questionnaire they completed, and no single questionnaire dominates the reference distribution by virtue of sample size. This transformation is based on nonlinear functions of the score (they follow whatever shape each questionnaire's empirical distribution actually has) and goes beyond a pure mean shift. Full mathematical details and explanation of each step are provided in the Supplementary Material S4. Supplementary Material S5 shows the effect of the score transformation pipeline on the underlying distributions of the data. The development of our score-transformation pipeline was inspired by established distributionalalignment principles from other harmonization contexts, including equipercentile equating in the psychometric test-linking literature 33, 34 , quantile normalization approaches used to harmonize measurements across batches in genomics 35 , and batch-effect harmonization methods such as ComBat 36.

All analyses used data from the ENIGMA-PD working group, comprising 4,881 participants who completed 19 different questionnaires (20,355 participant-questionnaire observations; data from 64 unique international sites). An additional independent cohort from the Department of Psychiatry, Psychosomatic Medicine and Psychotherapy, University Hospital Frankfurt, Frankfurt am Main, Germany (FRAME-DT study cohort, 84 patients with a diagnosis of Major Depressive Disorder (MDD), 25 healthy controls (HC), 6 questionnaires) served as an external validation dataset. This cohort was not used in any stage of pipeline development: of its six questionnaires, only one (the BDI) overlaps with the ENIGMA-PD instrument set, so validation on the remaining five (HAMD, MADRS, IDS-CR, PHQ-9, GHQ-28) constitutes a genuine test of generalization to previously unseen instruments, not merely unseen participants. We further included this cohort to extend our approach beyond PD to a primary psychiatric population: many of the questionnaires harmonized in the ENIGMA-PD sample were originally developed and validated in psychiatric rather than neurological samples, making an MDD cohort a natural generalizability target for neuropsychiatric constructs. Because psychiatric symptoms in PD are confounded by motor, cognitive, and dopaminergic-medication effects that differentiate PD from HC on far more than psychopathology, the MDD-vs-HC contrast in Frankfurt also offers a cleaner known-groups validity check than is possible within ENIGMA-PD alone. Finally, as an independent clinical cohort collected outside the ENIGMA-PD consortium infrastructure, with its own site, language, and rater-training context, this dataset allows us to evaluate the effects of diagnosis, instrument, and site on our method under conditions distinct from those used to build it. Details for each cohort are provided in Supplementary Table T2.

Validation

We evaluated score transformation against the three pre-registered hypotheses (pre-registration: 10.17605http://osf.io/34CZW) and additional exploratory analyses.

Pre-registered hypotheses H1 -Cross-questionnaire equivalence: For each construct-dimension pair, we tested whether the averaged CDF score differed across questionnaires using analysis of covariance (ANCOVA), controlling for age, sex, site, group (PD vs. HC), language, LEDD, Hoehn & Yahr stage, disease duration and UPDRS-III total score, with questionnaire as the grouping factor.

H2 -Structural validity:

We ran CFA by questionnaire x construct x dimension, using the theoretical factor structure as the model.

H3 -Internal consistency:

We computed McDonald's ω for each questionnaire × dimension. We handled binary or ordinal items using tetrachoric correlations. Incremental R² (ΔR²) and the standardized regression coefficient were used as the primary indices of cross-instrument convergence.

External prediction:

We used the averaged CDF depression and anxiety scores from the FRAME-DT study cohort to predict WHODAS 2.0 total disability scores (n = 59). Model performance was evaluated using leave-one-out cross-validation (LOO-CV R²), which is appropriate for small samples and provides an approximately unbiased estimate of out-of-sample prediction accuracy.

Sensitivity analyses

To evaluate the robustness of the H1, H2, and H3 validation results to specific analytic choices, we repeated the full validation pipeline under seven covariate/subgroup specifications, applied independently to three versions of the harmonized dataset. The seven specifications were: ( 1) the full covariate set (age, sex, diagnostic group, disease duration, Hoehn & Yahr stage, levodopa-equivalent daily dose, and MDS-UPDRS Part III total score, plus site and language as categorical terms where available); (2) a reduced covariate set (age, sex, and diagnostic group only, plus site and language as categorical terms), included because the full covariate set restrict several analyses to a non-representative subsample; (3) and (4) the reduced covariate set restricted to healthy control participants only and to PD patients only, respectively, to assess whether questionnaire equivalence held separately within each diagnostic group; (5) and (6) the full-covariate-restricted HC-only and PD-only subsets; and (7) the reduced covariate set with language excluded from the model entirely, to isolate the contribution of language of administration to the estimated questionnaire effects.

Semantic Similarity Analysis

To automate questionnaire harmonization for novel items, we implemented a item-to-dimension semantic similarity approach with subsequent fine-tuning of embedding models and tests for generalization.

For each construct, we computed embeddings for both the dimension descriptions as well as the single items. Each questionnaire item was assigned to the dimension whose written description it most closely resembled, which directly mirrored the expert mapping task. We tested 14 embedding models, spanning general-purpose (bge-large 37 , e5-large 38 , instructor 39 , gtr-t5 40 , MPNet 41 , MiniLM 42 , SimCSE 43 , SBert-similarity 44 ), clinical (ClinicalBert 45 , Bio_ClinicalBert 45 , BioBert 46 , clinicalSentenceTransformer 44, 45 ), and mental-health-specific models (mentalroberta 47 , harmony 22 ). Each item was assigned to the dimension with maximum cosine similarity to that dimension's description. Model performance was evaluated against Round 1 expert assignments using Cohen's κ. Model confidence was quantified analogously to expert confidence as 𝑀_𝑃_𝑖 = 𝛴 𝑀²ᵢₖ, where 𝑀_𝑖𝑘 is the softmax-normalized similarity to dimension 𝑘.

Fine-Tuning

The best-performing model per construct was selected to learn clinical semantics from expert assignments (probability distribution of the assignments). Two model architectures were combined: (i) Prototype model: Dimension prototypes were computed as the weighted average of item embeddings, weighted by the expert soft-label distribution (𝐸_𝑖𝑘).

New items are assigned to the nearest prototype by cosine similarity.

Generalization

To assess generalizability, the fine-tuned models (trained on Round 1 expert assignments) were evaluated on an independent set of questionnaire items from Round 2 (347 items), expert-labeled during a separate annotation process. Five constructs were evaluated (depression, anxiety, psychosis, sleep, impulse control; apathy was omitted due to insufficient test items). The contrastive model was used for most constructs, the prototype model was used for impulse control. Agreement between model predictions and expert labels was assessed using Cohen's κ with 95% bootstrap confidence intervals (1,000 bootstrap samples). After the generalization assessment, items from Round 2 were added to the fine-tuning pool and models were re-trained.

All analyses were implemented in Python and are openly shared on our GitHub repository (https://github.com/julia-pfarr/ENIGMA-PD_questionnaire-harmonization). This is an observational, cross-sectional design using existing retrospective data for secondary analyses (https://enigma.ini.usc.edu/ongoing/enigma-parkinsons/). Blinding is not applicable to this computational harmonization study. As we used data from a multi-site retrospective consortium, missing data is not random. It is often structured, for example certain instruments not used at some sites, selective missingness in severe or cognitively impaired patients. Items with high missingness or low inter-site availability were excluded from the final harmonized dimension scores.

Discussion

We present a comprehensive protocol for retrospectively harmonizing a wide range of neuropsychiatric constructs and questionnaires when overlapping participant response data are unavailable. By mapping questionnaire items to shared symptom dimensions and transforming their scores onto a common reference scale, we reduced differences between instruments while retaining clinically relevant information. Harmonized scores captured diagnostic group differences and predicted scores from other questionnaires, with additional support from replication in an independent MDD cohort. However, the validation results were mixed: questionnaire differences remained in ENIGMA-PD, internal consistency varied, and most confirmatory factor models showed poor fit. Together, these findings suggest that the framework can support retrospective data integration, although the resulting scores should not be assumed to be interchangeable across all instruments.

For researchers wishing to combine questionnaires, this means that semantic mapping and score transformation offer a starting point, which still requires evaluation in the context of the intended analysis. In particular, researchers need to consider whether instruments cover comparable symptom content, whether the resulting dimensions are sufficiently reliable, and whether population differences could affect their interpretation. Our automated mapping models can help extend the framework to new questionnaires, having reproduced expert assignments with 85.3% accuracy overall. Nevertheless, agreement with expert mappings addresses a different question from whether the resulting scores provide valid and comparable measures.

Expert agreement and dimensional boundaries

Expert agreement was strongest for items describing concrete symptoms or behaviors, such as "I feel sad" (BDI_01, mood/affective), "Do you engage in activities specifically to continue gambling?" (QUIP-RS, pathological gambling), and "Visual hallucinations" (PPRS_01, hallucinations). Apathy, however, proved more difficult to map, which is consistent with the conceptual ambiguities described in the literature 48, 49 . Apathy items probing social engagement, such as "She has friends" (AES_12, P_i = 0.34) and "Do you have friends?" (LARS_08.1, P_i = 0.35), elicited responses from some raters that indicated reduced affective apathy, while others mapped these items to "does not fit", probably due to a lack of background information in these phrasings. Both questions were originally found to load on a factor that could be described as "(intellectual) curiosity and novelty seeking" 50, 51 , which may not align with our proposed dimensions and suggests a follow-up study that investigates apathy more carefully in the light of this dimensional framework. Similarly, in depression, hopelessness and death-wish items highlighted uncertainty about the boundary between affective and cognitive depression symptoms. We believe that this requires its own comprehensive discussion about whether items concerning suicidal content can be mapped to the current dimensions or if they require their own dimension(s).

Of 896 rated items, only 24 lacked a clear majority assignment and for most respondents disagreed about whether an item fit within any of our construct dimensions at all. For example, the open-ended NPI screening item "Does the patient have any other nighttime behaviors that bother you that we haven't talked about?" (NPI_K_08), had raters split between those categorizing it as nocturnal sleep disturbances and others marking it as "does not fit." Such items may support a screening context, but they lack specificity when investigating disorder dimensions and/or relating to brain imaging modalities. The real challenge lies in determining which questionnaires make sense to combine and which do not, a decision largely contingent upon the individual study and the extent of heterogeneity the investigator is willing to accept. Our expert consensus design offers a reference for semantic assignment without independently validating the dimensions themselves. Looking ahead, we recommend conducting a worldwide DELPHI method-based study to select dimensions in the future and achieve even greater harmony in construct dimensions.

Agreement decreased modestly in Round 2, when splitting the surveys led to panel sizes from 33-40 to 18-20 raters. Our simulations recommended 15-40 raters, depending on the construct and target agreement, which suggests that some Round 2 estimates may have been less precise (Supplementary Material S2). However, the items and panel composition also differed between rounds, so the reduction in agreement cannot be attributed to panel size alone.

What score validation tells us about comparability

The CDF transformation reduced differences in questionnaire means in both cohorts, although alignment remained incomplete in ENIGMA-PD. Many dimensions retained significant questionnaire effects after covariate adjustment, whereas no significant effects were detected across the five depression dimensions in FRAME-DT. Population and site heterogeneity may therefore have contributed to the remaining ENIGMA-PD differences. However, the cohorts also differed in instruments, diagnosis, sample size, and study design, which prevents us from isolating the contribution of any one factor. Moreover, the absence of significant questionnaire effects in FRAME-DT does not, by itself, establish equivalence.

Because distributional alignment is an intended consequence of the transformation, it cannot alone demonstrate that the aligned scores measure the same underlying latent factor. The additional validation analyses are therefore particularly relevant. Harmonized scores captured expected clinical group differences and predicted scores from other questionnaires for most eligible comparisons. They also predicted overall disability in FRAME-DT, suggesting that clinically useful information remained after transformation. This support was nevertheless stronger for some instruments and dimensions than for others.

The poor confirmatory factor-analysis results represent an important limitation. Only a minority of questionnaire models met conventional fit criteria in either cohort, which provides limited support for the proposed dimensional structure in participant responses. These dimensions were defined retrospectively and do not necessarily match the structures for which the instruments were originally developed. Although this may help explain the misfit, it does not resolve it. Sparse item coverage, missingness patterns, and estimation choices may also have contributed, but their effects would need to be examined directly. Thus, semantic agreement and score alignment should not be taken as evidence that the questionnaires share a common latent structure. Further structural validation, particularly in samples with broader overlapping questionnaire coverage, remains necessary.

Internal consistency showed a similarly mixed pattern. Most questionnaire-dimension pairs met the adequacy threshold, but several had low reliability. This may partly reflect the small number of mapped items or the breadth of symptom content within a dimension. However, limitations in the mappings or dimension definitions cannot be excluded. Researchers should therefore consider these findings when deciding which scores are sufficiently coherent for their planned analysis.

Convergent, discriminant, and cross-construct correlations followed the theoretically expected gradient on average, supporting the clinical validity of the dimension mapping. A notable exception was the depression association between GDS and MDS-UPDRS Part I, which was slightly negative, and STAI and MDS-UPDRS Part I showed essentially no within-person agreement for cognitive anxiety. This likely reflects the combined unreliability of comparing a single item against a multi-item scale together with differences in rater perspective (self-report versus clinician rating), though we cannot rule out a genuine mapping problem for this pair. As mentioned above, the decision ultimately lies with the investigator about how much heterogeneity in the latent information they accept for their study.

Rank preservation was strong, indicating that harmonization does not meaningfully distort individual differences even in dimensions where group-level equivalence is imperfect and the PD-HC effect-size pattern was nearly identical before and after transformation, which supports preservation of the clinical group signal. Sensitivity analyses also produced qualitatively similar conclusions under several restrictions. Taken together, these findings support the stability of the broad pattern, while leaving uncertainty about particular dimensions and instruments.

We also want to emphasize that we pooled data from 64 unique sites (PPMI is mentioned as one dataset, but in reality it includes data from 51 different sites), 19 different scales that also differ in assessment style (questionnaire, interview, caregiver perspective, etc.), 11 distinct numericscoring formats, and little to no overlapping participant response data across instruments. Most correction methods, transformations, or pooling protocols were developed for cases involving two different sites but the same data type, a single site with two different response formats but both on an integer scale, or multiple sites but all following the same assessment protocol [52] [53] [54] [55] . We are in the initial stages of extending all these approaches to more sites, more scaling differences (e.g. integer vs. ordinal), with less obvious or complex anchor variables. For example, using clinical variables such as "disease burden" as an anchor between datasets might seem plausible at first, but may also lack comparability across datasets or relevance to neuropsychiatric symptoms 12.

This represents one of the broader challenges that clinical assessment pooling must address, beyond the mathematical properties of the harmonization procedure itself. Against the degree of heterogeneity in our study, the ability to preserve key properties of the original data such as participant rank ordering, diagnostic group differences, cross-construct correlations, and crossquestionnaire predictive relationships after harmonization provides preliminary evidence for the validity and utility of the approach.

Beyond harmonization itself, since this study included a large number of questionnaires, this framework can be used to derive recommendations about which questionnaire best captures a given symptom dimension. As an illustration, fitting a single-factor CFA per construct-dimension pair (pooling every questionnaire's items for that dimension) in our ENIGMA-PD sample indicated that HADS is the "truest" measure of depression's mood and affective symptoms dimension (R² = .58), whereas BDI is the better indicator for its anxiety and distress dimension (R² = .32), compared to the other depression questionnaires included in this study. These rankings are sample-specific and may shift as data from other questionnaires is included. We therefore encourage other groups applying this approach to extract and share the CDF norms from their own samples, which the tool accompanying this paper (https://github.com/julia-pfarr/HarmoniCA) is designed to support. Moreover, our method can show which questionnaires are safe to pool and which differ too much to provide semantically equivalent measures.

Extending the framework through automated mapping

Whereas score validation concerns the interpretation of participant responses, the automated mapping analyses ask whether models can assign questionnaire items to the same dimensions as experts. In practice, automated assignments can reduce the work needed to extend the item inventory. A somewhat counterintuitive finding from the model-comparison stage is that generalpurpose sentence-embedding models consistently outperformed embedding models pretrained on clinical or mental-health-specific text. We interpret this as evidence that pretraining on clinical corpora such as electronic health records or biomedical literature does not straightforwardly transfer to the fine-grained semantic-matching task required here and that the general sentencesimilarity objectives used to train the better-performing models are more relevant priors for this task than domain-specific vocabulary knowledge. This finding may also interest other naturallanguage-processing applications in psychiatric research that default to domain-adapted embeddings without empirically comparing them with general-purpose alternatives.

No single embedding model performed best across all six constructs. A related exploratory analysis found that combining multiple models via majority-vote ensembling did not reliably outperform the single best per-construct model. Together, these results indicate that neither a "one model fits all constructs" nor a "more models are always better" heuristic held in our setting, and that empirical, construct-specific model selection was necessary to achieve optimal performance.

The practical claim of this work that a harmonization framework trained on one set of questionnaires can be applied to entirely new, previously unseen instruments is supported by strong held-out generalization performance. When evaluated on questionnaires that were not used during fine-tuning, models achieved an overall Cohen's κ of 0.817 (accuracy = 85.3%) against expert consensus, aggregated across the five constructs for which held-out test data were available. This level of agreement, considered almost perfect under conventional benchmarks, indicates that the semantic representations learned for the six neuropsychiatric constructs in this study transfer meaningfully beyond the specific questionnaires on which the models were trained.

Overall agreement with expert assignments on new items was high (κ = .817; accuracy = 85.3%), although performance varied across constructs. Sleep showed the weakest generalization result (κ = .468 [.125, .727]), which was based on only 14 held-out items and remains uncertain. For apathy, a hybrid configuration using base embeddings and nearest-neighbor retrieval outperformed the fine-tuned model, indicating that contrastive fine-tuning was not always the most effective approach.

The generalization estimates also require caution because the retrieval-prototype blending weight was selected using the same held-out set on which final performance was reported. This reuse may have produced optimistic estimates, which should be assessed using an additional independent test set. Larger and more diverse training and test samples would also help establish how well the models handle unfamiliar wording and dimensions with less distinct boundaries.

Broader clinical applications and outlook

The ENIGMA-PD application brought together 19 neuropsychiatric questionnaires from 64 sites, with varied response formats and assessment perspectives and little overlapping response data and an independent replication cohort, adding another disorder and 5 more questionnaires to the pool. With this unique combination we were able to show that the method can be applied across different diagnosis groups, different disease severity levels and different number of sites. Because it operationalizes symptom dimensions rather than diagnostic categories, it is also well suited to transdiagnostic research in the spirit of RDoC 56 and HiTOP 57 (e.g., harmonizing overlapping internalizing-spectrum instruments across depression, anxiety, OCD, and PTSD). With further validation in additional populations and clinical settings, it could also harmonize outcome measures across clinical trials and treatment-outcome studies, where different sites, phases, or eras of a trial program often rely on different scales (e.g., HAMD, MADRS, BDI, QIDS in depression trials).

For neuropsychiatric questionnaires, our framework requires no retraining when applied to a new clinical population. The same framework can be used to harmonize questionnaires in other domains after providing new training data. For example, the psychosis construct only included questionnaires measuring the two dimensions of hallucinations and delusions. To capture a broader spectrum of psychosis, formal thought disorder could also be incorporated 24 . Our companion tool, HarmoniCA (https://github.com/julia-pfarr/HarmoniCA), automates itemdimension mapping using our expert-survey inventory, fine-tuned models, and scoretransformation pipeline for new studies. Practically, this lets the framework serve as a standing, reusable resource rather than a one-time harmonization study.

Our method does not address other sources of between-sample heterogeneity, such as site effects, which remained large in this dataset even after controlling for demographic and disease variables and language. Future work should focus on expanding the dimension classifications, apply this method to other constructs, improve the score transformation approach (e.g., exploring a Bayesian approach for true severity sampling or investigating weighting schemes and cut-offs), and look at the effects of clinician-vs. patient-vs. caregiver-administered scales.

Our study highlights that content differences in questionnaires that supposedly measure the same construct should not be neglected. Investigating questionnaire-brain associations is already difficult, and questionnaire measurement heterogeneity adds another layer of complexity, uncertainty, and reduced comparability to this kind of analysis. A key strength of this study is its ground truth, which was established by a large panel of experts. Our results show that even after multiple data transformations, the clinical and practical utility is preserved, supporting the validity and reliability of the harmonization approach.

Figure 4.

Figure 4. The figure shows the full score transformation pipeline. Step 1: Two sets of different participants; one set takes Questionnaire A, the other set completes Questionnaire B. Step 2: Sum of the participant item scores for this dimension. Step 3: Instruments sample the severity spectrum differently, so the same proportion score may correspond to different percentile positions across questionnaires. Step 4a: This score reflects the participant's percentile rank among all participants who completed the same questionnaire. It enables comparison across subjects and is comparable within an instrument but not directly across instruments. Step 4b: Two participants with the same withinquestionnaire percentile rank receive the same av_cdf_score regardless of which questionnaire they completed.

Further analyses:

Clinical validity and practical utility Rank preservation: We assessed within-questionnaire rank preservation by computing Spearman correlations between each participant's proportion score and their averaged-CDF score, separately for each questionnaire-dimension pair. Nomological validity: We tested whether the averaged CDF score reproduced expected group differences (PD vs. HC; MDD vs. HC) using OLS regression models controlling for demographic covariates. Convergent/ discriminant validity: This analysis tests whether the dimensions produced by semantic mapping are both internally coherent across questionnaires (convergent) and separable from one another (discriminant). Convergent validity was assessed as the Pearson correlation between proportion scores from different questionnaires assigned to the same dimension; high correlations indicate that different questionnaires captured the same underlying construct. Discriminant validity was assessed as the Pearson correlation between proportion scores from questionnaires assigned to different dimensions; these correlations should be substantially lower than the convergent correlations if the dimensions are meaningfully distinct. Within-construct cross-questionnaire prediction (individual-level covariation): Where available, we used data from participants who completed two questionnaires per construct for within-construct cross-questionnaire prediction. We assessed cross-questionnaire prediction by regressing each questionnaire's averaged CDF score on the corresponding score from another questionnaire measuring the same dimension, controlling for age, sex, group, site, and language.

(

ii) Contrastive model: The embedding model was fine-tuned using contrastive learning (batch size 8, up to 5 epochs). Item pairs from the same dimension were pulled together; pairs from different dimensions were pushed apart. Hard negative mining was used to prioritize boundary cases (i.e., pairs that are semantically similar but belong to different dimensions). We used 𝐸_𝑖𝑘 as soft label training. Hyperparameters (learning rate, contrastive margin, hard-negative boost, percentile threshold) were optimized via a two-stage leave-one-group-out cross-validation (LOGO-CV) search. Both model types used a hybrid inference strategy combining prototype similarity (global structure) and k-nearest-neighbor similarity (local structure): final assignment 𝑠𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑒 = 𝛼 × 𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑦𝑝𝑒_𝑠𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑒 + (1 -𝛼) × 𝑘𝑁𝑁_𝑠𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑒, where α was optimized during hyperparameter search. The k-NN pool was initialized from the training items and can be extended as new validated items are added to the harmonized inventory.
